## Page 1


Verifiers and Generators:
Epistemic Semantics for Intuitionistic Logic
(Long Version)
Pablo Barenbaum
pbarenbaum@dc.uba.ar
Universidad Nacional de Quilmes (CONICET)
Bernal, Argentina
Departamento de Computación, Facultad de Ciencias Exactas y Naturales, Universidad de Buenos Aires
CABA, Argentina
Abstract
This paper explores epistemic realizability, a form of realizability in
which the property that a piece of data constitutes evidence for a
logical proposition is semi-decidable. In this framework, each propo-
sition 𝐴is assigned a verifier program ⟨𝐴⟩that checks whether a
datum 𝑋is a realizer for 𝐴, and a dual generator program ✠𝐴that
behaves as a generic realizer for 𝐴. We propose epistemic realizabil-
ity interpretations for minimal logic, second-order intuitionistic
logic, and higher-order intuitionistic logic, proving that each system
is sound and complete under the proposed semantics.
1
Introduction
Verificationism explains the meaning of logical connectives by
means of the notion of evidence rather than the notion of truth.
Under this view, the meaning of a proposition 𝐴is given by a
verification procedure to recognize evidence for 𝐴[15]. This work
explores the problem of providing a formal semantics for logical
connectives in an “epistemically acceptable” way. Formally, this
means that verification procedures must be effectively checkable:
the meaning of a proposition 𝐴must be a program 𝑃that computes
a partial computable predicate, such that 𝑃accepts 𝑋if it constitutes
evidence for 𝐴, and either rejects 𝑋or remains undefined otherwise.
We call this “epistemic semantics”, following Nelson [37, Sec. 6].
The primary challenge lies in providing epistemic semantics
for logical connectives. Standard semantic frameworks typically
do not provide effective means of recognizing evidence. For exam-
ple, classical model-theoretic semantics defines the meaning of a
universal quantifier ∀𝑥.𝑃(𝑥) by declaring that a structure satisfies
the formula if and only if it satisfies 𝑃(𝑎) for every element 𝑎in
the universe. While this definition is mathematically acceptable
—assuming a prior understanding of universal quantification in the
metalanguage— it is unsatisfactory from the perspective of epis-
temic semantics. Rather than providing an effective verification
procedure to determine whether an object is evidence for ∀𝑥.𝑃(𝑥),
the meaning of the universal quantifier is displaced from the object
language to the metatheory.
Realizability interpretations. Many constructive approaches to se-
mantics take BHK-style1 interpretations as their starting point [52].
These interpretations align with verificationism by defining the
meaning of a proposition through its realizers (also referred to
as constructions, proofs, evidence, etc.). One formal incarnation of
1BHK stands for Brouwer–Heyting–Kolmogorov.
these ideas is Kleene’s realizability [29], in which the meaning of
conjunction is defined as:
⟨𝑛,𝑚⟩⊩(𝐴∧𝐵)
def
⇐⇒𝑛⊩𝐴and 𝑚⊩𝐵
where ⟨𝑛,𝑚⟩represents Gödel’s pairing function and 𝑛⊩𝐴denotes
that the natural number 𝑛is a realizer for 𝐴. This definition declares
that a realizer for a conjunction 𝐴∧𝐵is given by a pair containing
a realizer for 𝐴and a realizer for 𝐵. Moreover, it is epistemically
acceptable, in the sense that it provides a decision procedure: to
verify that a pair ⟨𝑛,𝑚⟩is a realizer for 𝐴∧𝐵, one can recursively
verify that 𝑛is a realizer for 𝐴and 𝑚is a realizer for 𝐵.
Troublesome connectives. Unfortunately, BHK-style interpreta-
tions do not provide epistemically acceptable definitions for all
connectives. In particular, the semantics of “troublesome” operators
—implication, negation, universal quantification— is expressed in
terms of meta-level universal quantification. For instance, the mean-
ing of implication in Kleene’s realizability is defined as follows:
𝑛⊩(𝐴⇒𝐵)
def
⇐⇒∀𝑚∈N. (𝑚⊩𝐴=⇒𝜑𝑛(𝑚) ⊩𝐵)
where 𝜑𝑛denotes the 𝑛-th partial computable function. This defini-
tion declares that a realizer for an implication 𝐴⇒𝐵is given by a
partial computable function 𝜑𝑛that transforms every realizer for 𝐴
into a realizer for 𝐵. This is a priori not epistemically acceptable,
because to verify that 𝑛is a realizer for 𝐴⇒𝐵, one would have to
semi-decide a universal quantification ∀𝑚∈N.(...) over the natural
numbers, which is in general an impossible task2.
The formalist escape route. As discussed above, the intuitive
“meaning” of certain connectives, specifically implication, nega-
tion, and universal quantification, seems difficult to reconcile with
an epistemic semantics. One way to achieve this reconciliation
is through formalism. Formalism can be synthesized as the view
that evidence for a proposition 𝐴is provided by a derivation of 𝐴
in a specific formal proof system. Any proof system that has the
property of semi-decidable proof-checking immediately provides a
trivial epistemic semantics for propositions. Indeed, the verification
procedure is given by the proof-checker itself, and the string of
symbols encoding the derivation serves as the required existential
witness. Formalism allows us to understand mathematical activity
2The meaning of implication in the object language depends on the meaning of uni-
versal quantification in the metatheory. Note that there are ways to understand the
universal formula 𝜓≡∀𝑚∈N.(...) in an epistemically acceptable way; for example,
as the existence of a derivation of𝜓in Peano arithmetic. (See the subsequent comments
on formalism).

## Page 2


Verifiers and Generators: Epistemic Semantics for Intuitionistic Logic
P. Barenbaum
as a combinatorial game of producing derivations, but leaves open
the question of whether propositions can be given any other mean-
ing beyond the symbols themselves. For example, in the absence of
semantics, proof systems appear as entirely arbitrary constructions,
and it is impossible to even wonder whether they are sound or
complete.
Epistemic realizability. This work studies a epistemic realiz-
ability semantics for logical propositions, inspired by BHK-style
interpretations and traditional realizability, but formulated from
the perspective of epistemic semantics. Early works on realizability
were formulated in the language of arithmetic, so data types other
than integers had to be handled through explicit Gödel codings.
In this paper we use —as other works also do— extensions of the
untyped 𝜆-calculus as the language to express both programs and
data3.
The interpretations we propose center on the dual notions of
verifiers and generators. We begin by interpreting each proposition
𝐴as a program called its verifier and written ⟨𝐴⟩. The verifier
for 𝐴is an untyped 𝜆-term that computes a partial computable
predicate. We say that 𝑋is a realizer for 𝐴if, by definition, ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑋)
succeeds. Success, in this framework, is defined by the property
that ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑋) ↠★, where ↠denotes reduction in zero or more steps
and ★is a constant representing successful termination. Note that
the input 𝑋is itself expressed using an untyped 𝜆-term.
For example, the meaning of a conjunction 𝐴∧𝐵is a program
⟨𝐴∧𝐵⟩such that ⟨𝐴∧𝐵⟩(𝑋) succeeds if both ⟨𝐴⟩(𝜋1(𝑋)) and
⟨𝐵⟩(𝜋2(𝑋)) succeed, where 𝜋1(𝑋) and 𝜋2(𝑋) are the first and sec-
ond projections of a pair, in accordance with the BHK interpretation.
Verifiers and generators. A significant difficulty arises when at-
tempting to define the meaning of “troublesome” connectives. For
example, implication is challenging due to the reversal of polarity
in the antecedent. According to the BHK interpretation, the verifier
⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩must take an input 𝑋and determine whether, for every 𝑌
such that ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑌) succeeds, the application ⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋𝑌) also succeeds.
This verification is not decidable a priori. Indeed, the predicate:
𝑃(𝑋)
≡
∀𝑌.(⟨𝐴⟩(𝑌) ↠★=⇒⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋𝑌) ↠★)
starts with a universal quantifier. This is not a priori semi-decidable,
so it is unclear how to define a program ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩that computes 𝑃.
To resolve this, we refine the interpretation: now each proposi-
tion 𝐴is interpreted by both its verifier ⟨𝐴⟩and a second program,
called its generator and written ✠𝐴. The generator for 𝐴is an un-
typed 𝜆-term that behaves as a generic realizer for 𝐴. For example,
in the case of conjunction, ✠𝐴∧𝐵behaves as the pair (✠𝐴, ✠𝐵),
also in accordance with the BHK interpretation.
From a computer science perspective, a verifier ⟨𝐴⟩is a program
that tests whether its argument complies with a specification𝐴. Con-
versely, a generator ✠𝐴serves as a stub, or a “mock implementation”
that behaves as if it were compliant with said specification. One
basic property of this system is correctness: a verifier must accept its
corresponding generator as a valid realizer, that is, ⟨𝐴⟩(✠𝐴) ↠★.
Aided by the presence of generators, we can define the verifier
for an implication in such a way that ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩(𝑋) succeeds if and
only if ⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋✠𝐴) succeeds. That is, to determine whether 𝑋is a
3This choice is not essential but quite convenient.
realizer for 𝐴⇒𝐵, we check whether 𝑋produces a realizer for 𝐵
when supplied with the realizer for 𝐴produced by the generator.
To complete the picture, we need to define the generator for
an implication 𝐴⇒𝐵. Verifiers and generators are defined in a
mutually inductive fashion. Indeed, the generator ✠𝐴⇒𝐵is a pro-
gram that takes an arbitrary input 𝑋and returns ✠𝐵provided
that ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑋) succeeds. Formally, this can be written as ✠𝐴⇒𝐵=
𝜆𝑥. (⟨𝐴⟩𝑥?✠𝐵). Here 𝜆𝑥. (...) is the standard 𝜆-notation for anony-
mous functions, and 𝑀?𝑁is an operator that proceeds to evaluate
𝑁provided that the evaluation of 𝑀terminates successfully.
Scope of the work and propositions-as-types. We study epistemic
realizability across three incrementally more expressive systems
of intuitionistic logic: 1. minimal logic; 2. second-order intu-
itionistic logic; and 3. higher-order intuitionistic logic. As is
well-known, derivations of sequents in natural deduction systems
correspond to terms in typed 𝜆-calculi, through the propositions-
as-types correspondence (cf. for instance [25, 49]). The formal de-
ductive systems that we study are three standard typed 𝜆-calculi
corresponding to each of the logics above, namely: 1. simply typed
𝜆-calculus (𝜆→); 2. polymorphic 𝜆-calculus (System F [22, 44]);
and 3. higher-order polymorphic 𝜆-calculus (System F𝜔[22]).
Contributions. For each of the three aforementioned typed 𝜆-
calculi, we define a corresponding untyped 𝜆-calculus, called the
metacalculus. For example, for the simply typed 𝜆-calculus (𝜆→)
we define a metacalculus (𝜆→
𝔪) that allows to express both verifiers
⟨𝐴⟩and generators ✠𝐴for every proposition 𝐴in the language of
minimal logic. For each system, the main result is that the logic
is both sound and complete with respect to the epistemic realiz-
ability interpretation. This establishes that 𝐴is a theorem in the
logical theory if and only if there exists evidence for 𝐴in the meta-
calculus, that is, an untyped term 𝑋such that ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑋) succeeds. For
completeness to hold, we further require that the evidence 𝑋must
be a normalizing and pure term; that is, it must have a normal form
and not contain extraneous elements such as generators ✠𝐴.
While relating typed 𝜆-calculi and epistemic realizability may ap-
pear straightforward, proving soundness and completeness presents
significant technical challenges. For example, proving soundness of
the modus ponens rule, requires showing that if both ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩(𝑋)
and ⟨𝐴⟩(𝑌) succeed then ⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋𝑌) must also succeed. In traditional
realizability, this is precisely the definition of implication, leaving
nothing to prove. In our “epistemic” setting, however, the condition
that ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩(𝑋) succeeds only guarantees that 𝑋behaves cor-
rectly when applied to the generator ✠𝐴. Consequently, one must
prove that ⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋✠𝐴) ↠★and ⟨𝐴⟩𝑌↠★imply ⟨𝐵⟩(𝑋𝑌) ↠★.
Establishing this property is a non-trivial task, as it requires to
show that in some sense 𝑋cannot distinguish between the actual
realizer 𝑌and the “mock” realizer produced by the generator ✠𝐴.
Structure of the paper. In Sec. 2 we review some preliminary no-
tions and fix some of the notations used in the rest of the paper.
In Sec. 3 we study epistemic realizability for minimal logic. In Sec. 4
we extend the results to second-order logic. This requires to gener-
alize the notions of verifier and generator to deal with second-order
universal quantifiers. The proof techniques also have to be adapted,
to address the usual difficulties posed by impredicativity. In Sec. 5
we discuss the extension to higher-order logic. Finally, in Sec. 6 we

conclude, and discuss some potential applications in programming
languages and proof assistants, as well as related and future work.
2
Preliminaries
In this section we recall some standard notions, to fix nomenclature
and notation.
We work with various typed and untyped 𝜆-calculi, all of them
instances of abstract rewriting systems (ARSs). An ARS is a pair
(𝑋, →), where 𝑋is a set of elements called objects, and →is a binary
relation →⊆𝑋× 𝑋. Standard references are [2, 50] for rewriting
systems and [3, 5] for the 𝜆-calculus. If 𝑅⊆𝑋×𝑌and 𝑆⊆𝑌×𝑍are
binary relations, we write 𝑅◦𝑆(or just 𝑅𝑆) for their composition,
defined by {(𝑥,𝑧) ∈𝑋× 𝑍| ∃𝑦∈𝑌. 𝑥𝑅𝑦∧𝑦𝑅𝑧}. If (𝑋, →) is
an ARS, we write →0 for the diagonal relation and →𝑛+1 := (→
◦→𝑛). We write →= for the reflexive closure (i.e. →= = →0 ∪→);
→−1 or ←for the inverse relation (i.e. ←= {(𝑦,𝑥) | 𝑥→𝑦}); →∗
or ↠for the reflexive–transitive closure (i.e. ↠= ∪𝑛≥0 →𝑛); ↔
for the symmetric closure (i.e. ↔= ←∪→); and ↞↠for reflexive–
symmetric–transitive closure (i.e. ↞↠= ↔∗). Given a relation →𝑋,
we write =𝑋for ↞↠𝑋, and call it 𝑋-convertibility. Given relations
→𝑋and →𝑌, we write →𝑋𝑌for their union.
An object 𝑥is →-irreducible or a →-normal form if there is no
𝑦such that 𝑥→𝑦. An object 𝑥is →-normalizing if there exists a
normal form 𝑦such that 𝑥↠𝑦. An ARS is deterministic if for all
objects 𝑥,𝑦,𝑦′ such that 𝑥→𝑦and 𝑥→𝑦′ one has that 𝑦= 𝑦′. An
ARS has the diamond property if ←→⊆→←, it is subcommutative
if ←→⊆→= ←=, and it is confluent or Church–Rosser if ↞↠⊆
↠↞. Determinism, the diamond property, and subcommutativity
are each stronger properties than confluence. The relations →1 and
→2 commute if ↞1 ↠2 ⊆↠2 ↞1.
In the concrete rewriting systems that we use, objects are ex-
pressions (𝑀, 𝑁,𝐴, 𝐵,𝑡,𝑠, . . .) of various sorts (terms, types, etc.).
Expressions may contain occurrences of variables (𝑥,𝑦,𝑎,𝑏, . . .) of
various sorts (variables, type-variables, etc.). Some syntactic con-
structors may bind variables, e.g. a 𝜆-abstraction 𝜆𝑥.𝑡binds the
occurrences of 𝑥in 𝑡. The notions of free and bound occurrences of
a variable are defined as expected in each case. We work implicitly
up to 𝛼-renaming of bound variables. We write fv(𝐸) to denote the
set of free variables of the expression 𝐸. We write 𝐸1{𝑥:= 𝐸2} for
the expression that results from performing the capture-avoiding
substitution of all the free occurrences of 𝑥in 𝐸1 by 𝐸2. Sometimes
we work with substitutions 𝜎, which are functions mapping vari-
ables to expressions. We write 𝜎[𝑥↦→𝐸] for the extension of the
substitution 𝜎with the mapping 𝑥↦→𝐸, i.e. the substitution 𝜎′ such
that 𝜎′(𝑦) = 𝐸if 𝑥= 𝑦, and 𝜎′(𝑦) = 𝜎(𝑦) otherwise. We write 𝐸𝜎to
denote the expression that results from the parallel substitution of
each free occurrence of each free variable 𝑥in 𝐸by 𝜎(𝑥), avoiding
capture. The usual properties of substitution apply in these cases,
e.g. 𝐸1{𝑥:= 𝐸2}𝜎= 𝐸𝜎
1 {𝑥:= 𝐸𝜎
2 } holds assuming that 𝑥is safe for
𝜎, i.e. 𝑥∉fv(𝜎(𝑦)) for every 𝑦. Sometimes we use these standard
syntactical facts without providing explicit proofs.
In these systems we usually consider contexts, which are expres-
sions with a single occurrence of a distinguished free variable □,
called the hole. If C is a context and 𝐸is an expression, we write
C[𝐸] for the expression that results from “plugging” 𝐸into the hole
of C, possibly capturing variables.
3
Epistemic Realizability for Minimal Logic
In this section, we develop a epistemic realizability semantics for
minimal logic, the intuitionistic system that includes implica-
tion as the single connective. The main results in this section are
soundness (Thm. 3.11) and completeness (Thm. 3.14).
The simply typed 𝜆-calculus (𝜆→). We begin by recalling the
typed 𝜆-calculus that corresponds to minimal logic from the per-
spective of the propositions-as-types correspondence, the (Curry-
style) simply typed 𝜆-calculus. We assume given a denumerable set
of atomic types (a, b, . . .) and variables (𝑥,𝑦, . . .). The sets of types
and terms of 𝜆→are given by:
Types
𝐴, 𝐵, . . .
::=
a | 𝐴⇒𝐵
Terms
𝑡,𝑠, . . .
::=
𝑥| 𝜆𝑥.𝑡| 𝑡𝑠
An environment, ranged over by Γ, Δ, . . ., is a finite set of type
assignments {𝑥1 : 𝐴1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛: 𝐴𝑛}, where 𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛are distinct
variables. We write dom(Γ) for the set of variables that appear in Γ.
Typing judgements are of the form Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴, and defined inductively
by the following rules:
Γ,𝑥: 𝐴⊢𝑥: 𝐴
Γ,𝑥: 𝐴⊢𝑡: 𝐵
Γ ⊢𝜆𝑥.𝑡: 𝐴⇒𝐵
Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴⇒𝐵
Γ ⊢𝑠: 𝐴
Γ ⊢𝑡𝑠: 𝐵
The binary relations of 𝛽and 𝜂-reduction are defined by the follow-
ing reduction rules, closed under arbitrary contexts:
(𝜆𝑥.𝑡) 𝑠→𝛽𝑡{𝑥:= 𝑠}
𝜆𝑥.𝑡𝑥→𝜂𝑡
if 𝑥∉fv(𝑡)
Both notions of reduction are defined over untyped terms, but they
can be restricted to typable terms, using the well-known property
that they preserve types (i.e. subject reduction holds).
The metacalculus (𝜆→
𝔪). Next, we define the metacalculus in
which we will be able to formulate the notions of verifiers, genera-
tors, realizers, and so on, for minimal logic. The metacalculus 𝜆→
𝔪
is an extension of the untyped 𝜆-calculus. It would be possible to
formulate the semantics using the (unextended) 𝜆-calculus directly,
but working in an extension is clearer, and more convenient, as it
avoids the accidental complexities of encodings.
To emphasize their specific role, and to avoid confusion with
𝜆→-terms, terms of the metacalculus are called metaterms. The set
of metaterms of 𝜆→

We use the constant ★to represent that a computation succeeds. The
guard 𝑀?𝑁can be used to check whether 𝑀succeeds and return 𝑁
if this is the case. A metaterm of the form ✠a represents a generic
realizer for the atomic type a, while a metaterm of the form ⟨a⟩𝑀
intuitively “checks” whether 𝑀is a realizer for the atomic type a.
We adopt the convention that the guard has higher precedence
than the abstraction, but lower precedence than the application
and the verifier, so for example 𝜆𝑥. ⟨a⟩𝑀?𝑁𝑃is equivalent to
𝜆𝑥. ((⟨a⟩𝑀)?(𝑁𝑃)).
A metaterm is pure if it only involves variables, abstractions,
and applications. Terms of 𝜆→are identified with pure metaterms
of 𝜆→
𝔪. Note that reduction in 𝜆→
𝔪over pure terms is just standard
→𝛽-reduction. A metaterm is good if it is pure and →𝛽-normalizing.
Interpretation of types. The metacalculus 𝜆→
𝔪includes generators
and verifiers only for atomic types. We extend them to arbitrary
types 𝐴by providing mutually recursive definitions of the generator
✠𝐴that produces a generic realizer of 𝐴, and the verifier ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀
that checks whether its argument 𝑀is a realizer for 𝐴.
Definition 3.1 (Generators and verifiers for 𝜆→
𝔪). Let 𝐴be a type.
The generator for 𝐴, written ✠𝐴, and the verifier for 𝐴with ar-
gument 𝑀, written ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀, are metaterms defined as follows by
induction on 𝐴. In the base case, when 𝐴= a is an atomic type, the
definition coincides with the metaterm constructors in the grammar
(✠a and ⟨a⟩𝑀). In the recursive case:
✠𝐴⇒𝐵
:=
𝜆𝑥. (⟨𝐴⟩𝑥?✠𝐵)
⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩𝑀
:=
⟨𝐵⟩(𝑀✠𝐴)
The definition of ✠𝐴⇒𝐵intuitively means that, to generate
a generic realizer for 𝐴⇒𝐵, we may construct an abstraction
𝜆𝑥. (⟨𝐴⟩𝑥?✠𝐵) that checks whether its argument is a realizer for
𝐴, and, if this succeeds, generates a realizer for 𝐵. The definition of
⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩𝑀intuitively means that, to verify whether 𝑀is a realizer
for 𝐴⇒𝐵, we provide 𝑀with a generic realizer for 𝐴, and check
whether the result is a realizer for 𝐵.
Formally, we say that 𝑀is a realizer for 𝐴if ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀↠★, and we
say that 𝐴is realizable if it has a good realizer.
Note on syntax. The syntax for verifiers requires ⟨𝐴⟩to always be
accompanied by its argument 𝑀. This is just a syntactic constraint
that helps simplify some proofs. If the verifier needs to be treated
as a stand-alone operator it can be defined via an 𝜂-like expansion
as ⟨𝐴⟩:= 𝜆𝑥. ⟨𝐴⟩𝑥.
Example 3.2 (The𝐾and𝑆combinators realize Hilbert-style axioms).
The following reduction shows that the 𝐾combinator (𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝑥)
realizes the first axiom (a ⇒b ⇒a) in the Hilbert-style system for
minimal logic:
⟨a ⇒b ⇒a⟩(𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝑥)
=
⟨b ⇒a⟩((𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝑥) ✠a)
=
⟨a⟩((𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝑥) ✠a ✠b)
↠
⟨a⟩✠a →★
The 𝑆combinator (𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝜆𝑧. 𝑥𝑧(𝑦𝑧)), in turn, realizes the second
axiom ((a ⇒b ⇒c) ⇒(a ⇒b) ⇒a ⇒c):
⟨(a ⇒b ⇒c) ⇒(a ⇒b) ⇒a ⇒c⟩(𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝜆𝑧. 𝑥𝑧(𝑦𝑧))

⟨c⟩((𝜆𝑥. 𝜆𝑦. 𝜆𝑧. 𝑥𝑧(𝑦𝑧)) ✠a⇒b⇒c ✠a⇒b ✠a)
↠

=
⟨c⟩((𝜆𝑥. ⟨a⟩𝑥?✠b⇒c) ✠a (✠a⇒b ✠a))
→

⟨c⟩(✠b⇒c (✠a⇒b ✠a))
↠
⟨c⟩((𝜆𝑥. ⟨b⟩𝑥?✠c)(✠a⇒b ✠a))
↠
⟨c⟩(⟨b⟩(✠a⇒b ✠a)?✠c) ↠. . . →★
The two following examples illustrate applications of epistemic
realizability. Note that these are well-known results that can be
established through other methods, such as standard realizability
interpretations.
Example 3.3 (Consistency). It straightforward to show that not
every type is realizable. Indeed, let a be an arbitrary atomic type
and suppose that ⟨a⟩𝑀↠★for a pure metaterm 𝑀. Inspecting the
rewriting rules of 𝜆→
𝔪, it is clear that this could only be possible if
there were a reduction 𝑀↠✠a. But 𝑀is pure and contains no
generators, and it is straighforward to show that reduction cannot
create generators, so we obtain a contradiction. Combining this
with soundness (Thm. 3.11), we conclude that not every formula is
a theorem in minimal logic; that is, the logic is consistent.
Example 3.4 (Specification results). The interpretation can be
used to characterize the inherent computational behaviour of the
realizers of a given type. For example, we claim that if 𝑀is a pure
term that realizes a ⇒a, then 𝑀=𝛽𝜆𝑥. 𝑥. To see this (informally),
suppose that ⟨a ⇒a⟩𝑀↠★. By definition, we have ⟨a⟩(𝑀✠a) ↠
★. This can only happen if 𝑀✠a ↠✠a. But 𝑀is a pure term
without verifiers, and reduction cannot spontaneously create a
verifier ⟨a⟩. So ✠a can be understood as just an “inert” symbol that
does not interact with 𝑀, and it can be replaced by a fresh variable
𝑥to obtain a reduction 𝑀𝑥↠𝑥. Since reduction in 𝜆→
𝔪over pure
terms is →𝛽-reduction, it follows that 𝑀𝑥=𝛽𝑥, which is easily seen
to imply 𝑀=𝛽𝜆𝑥. 𝑥. Combining this with soundness (Thm. 3.11),
one obtains that the unique realizer of a ⇒a is the identity. This is
a classic result, dating back at least to Reynolds [45].
Rewriting properties of 𝜆→
𝔪. Before going on, we mention some
basic syntactic results that are necessary to show that the interpre-
tation is sound and complete. First, it is straightforward to show
that there is at most one weak head redex (see Sec. A.1). Hence:
Proposition 3.5. Weak head reduction →𝑤is deterministic.
Moreover, 𝜆→
𝔪is an orthogonal higher-order rewriting system in
the sense of Nipkow [38], which entails:
Proposition 3.6. Reduction in 𝜆→
𝔪is confluent.
A further rewriting property that we need is that reductions end-
ing in ★can be standardized to weak head reductions. Compare this
property with the well-known fact that the head reduction strategy
is head normalizing in the 𝜆-calculus (see e.g. [3, Coro. 11.4.8]). We
do not include an explicit proof of this result, but Sec. 4 proves an
analogous fact for the (more difficult) second-order case:
Proposition 3.7 (Standardization). If 𝑀↠★then 𝑀↠𝑤★.

3.1
Soundness of 𝜆→with respect to 𝜆→
𝔪
Our goal is to show that the typed calculus 𝜆→is sound and com-
plete with respect to the verification semantics induced by the
metacalculus 𝜆→
𝔪. In this section we focus on soundness, which
states that all theorems of minimal logic are realizable. More pre-
cisely, whenever 𝑡is a proof of 𝐴, i.e. a closed term 𝑡such that ⊢𝑡: 𝐴
holds, then 𝑡is also a realizer4 for 𝐴, that is, ⟨𝐴⟩𝑡↠★. Moreover,
𝑡is clearly good: it is pure by construction, and the standard fact
that simply typed terms are strongly normalizing implies that 𝑡is
𝛽-normalizing.
Stating soundness for open terms. To be able to reason induc-
tively over the structure of terms, we are compelled to generalize
the statement of soundness to open terms, i.e. terms that contain
free variables. The generalized version states that if 𝑥1 : 𝐴1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛:
𝐴𝑛⊢𝑡: 𝐵holds, then to obtain a realizer for 𝐵one should re-
place each variable 𝑥𝑖by the generator ✠𝐴𝑖. Formally, one has that
⟨𝐵⟩𝑡{𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛:= ✠𝐴1, . . . , ✠𝐴𝑛} ↠★.
Substitutions and generative substitutions. A substitution (𝜎, 𝜎′, . . .)
is a function mapping variables to metaterms. Recall that we write
𝑀𝜎for the result of applying the substition 𝜎on each free variable
of 𝑀, avoiding capture.
The generative substitution for an environment Γ is written Γ and
defined as the substitution such that Γ(𝑥) = ✠𝐴holds for every
𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ, and Γ(𝑥) = 𝑥holds for every 𝑥∉dom(Γ). By abuse of
notation, sometimes we write just Γ for the generative substitution,
rather than Γ. For example, if Γ = (𝑥: 𝐴,𝑦: 𝐵) then:
((𝜆𝑥. 𝑥𝑦𝑦) 𝑥)Γ = (𝜆𝑥. 𝑥✠𝐵✠𝐵) ✠𝐴
Using generative substitutions, soundness can be refined to state
that Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐵implies ⟨𝐵⟩𝑡Γ ↠★.
Correctness and universality. The proofs of soundness and com-
pleteness that we provide in this section are “syntactic” in nature,
as they rely only on rewriting techniques and elementary structural
induction over the size of expressions. We anticipate that the tran-
sition to second and higher-order logic in the following sections
shall require a different approach.
The proof of soundness of minimal logic relies on the two fol-
lowing key properties:
1. Correctness. The generator ✠𝐴is accepted by the verifier
⟨𝐴⟩, that is, ⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴↠★.
2. Universality. The generator ✠𝐴is “universal” with respect
to the verifier ⟨𝐴⟩. More precisely, if 𝑃and 𝑀are metaterms
such that 𝑃accepts the generator ✠𝐴, and the verifier ⟨𝐴⟩
accepts 𝑀, then 𝑃accepts 𝑀. Schematically:
𝑃✠𝐴↠★
⟨𝐴⟩𝑀↠★
𝑃𝑀↠★
These two conditions together state that verifier/generator pairs
enjoy a sort of “universal property”5. Correctness is easy to show:
Lemma 3.8 (Correctness). ⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴↠★

3.2
Completeness of 𝜆→with respect to 𝜆→

Completeness is the statement that realizable types are provable, i.e.
that if 𝑀is a good metaterm such that ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀↠★then ⊢𝐴holds
in minimal logic. As we did for soundness, we have to generalize
the statement in order to account for possibly open terms.
The generalized statement of completeness says that if 𝑀is a
good metaterm such that ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀Γ ↠★then there exists a term 𝑡
such that Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴. Moreover, 𝑡is such that 𝑀=𝛽𝑡. In fact, 𝑡can
be chosen to be the 𝛽-normal form of 𝑀.
Measuring the size of realizers. The proof of completeness pro-
ceeds by induction on an integer |𝑀|Γ⊢𝐴called the proof size. It is
defined for any pure metaterm 𝑀, even if it is untypable, assum-
ing that 𝑀has a 𝛽-normal form. Recall the well-known fact that
the following grammar of normal terms characterizes exactly the
set of terms in 𝛽-normal form (which coincides with the set of pure
metaterms in normal form):

## Page 7


Verifiers and Generators: Epistemic Semantics for Intuitionistic Logic
P. Barenbaum
4
Extension to Second-Order Logic
One way to extend the epistemic realizability interpretation from
the previous section would be to define verifiers and generators for
additional propositional connectives. For example, one might define
the verifier for conjunction as ⟨𝐴∧𝐵⟩𝑀= ⟨𝐴⟩𝜋1(𝑀)?⟨𝐵⟩𝜋2(𝑁),
and the generator as ✠𝐴∧𝐵= (✠𝐴, ✠𝐵). However, it is not imme-
diately obvious how to define verifiers and generators for positive
connectives such as disjunction (𝐴∨𝐵).
We take a different route, and extend the interpretation from
minimal logic to second-order logic, incorporating second-order
universal quantification. It is well-known that other logical con-
nectives (including the intuitionistic propositional connectives), as
well as inductive datatypes (natural numbers, lists, trees, etc.) can
be expressed in second-order logic, e.g. using Böhm–Berarducci
encodings [6].
The main results in this section are soundness (Thm. 4.12) and
completeness (Thm. 4.14). While the proof of completeness is an
adaptation of the one for minimal logic, the proof of soundness
requires a fundamentally different approach.
The second-order 𝜆-calculus (𝜆F). We begin by recalling the typed
𝜆-calculus corresponding to second-order logic, namely Girard–
Reynolds System F [22, 44]. We assume given disjoint denumerable
sets of type-variables (𝑎,𝑏, . . .), and eigenvariables (a, b, . . .). The sets
of types and terms are given by the grammar:
Types
𝐴, 𝐵, . . .
::=
𝑎| a | 𝐴⇒𝐵| ∀𝑎. 𝐴
Terms
𝑡,𝑠, . . .
::=
𝑥| 𝜆𝑥.𝑡| 𝑡𝑠| Λ𝑎.𝑡| 𝑡𝐴
The (second-order) universal quantifier ∀𝑎. 𝐴binds the occurrences
of 𝑎in 𝐴. Eigenvariables are similar to type-variables, but they are
assumed to never occur bound in types or terms (although it will
be possible that they occur bound in metaterms). A type is atomic
if it is either a type-variable or an eigenvariable.
From the propositions-as-types point of view, 𝜆𝑥.𝑡and𝑡𝑠encode
introduction and elimination of the implication, while Λ𝑎.𝑡and 𝑡𝐴
encode introduction and elimination of the second-order universal
quantifier. Typing judgments are defined with the three typing rules
for 𝜆→given in the previous section, plus the two following rules:
Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴

Γ ⊢Λ𝑎.𝑡: ∀𝑎. 𝐴
Γ ⊢𝑡: ∀𝑎. 𝐴
Γ ⊢𝑡𝐵: 𝐴{𝑎:= 𝐵}
We define 𝛽and 𝜂-reduction as the union of the following rules,
closed by arbitrary contexts:
(𝜆𝑥.𝑡) 𝑠→𝛽𝑡{𝑥:= 𝑠}
(Λ𝑎.𝑡) 𝐴→𝛽𝑡{𝑎:= 𝐴}
𝜆𝑥.𝑡𝑥→𝜂𝑡if 𝑥∉fv(𝑡)
Λ𝑎.𝑡𝑎→𝜂𝑡if 𝑎∉fv(𝑡)
Both notions of reduction are defined over arbitrary terms, even
if they are not typable, but they can also be restricted to typable
terms, using the well-known property that they preserve types.

A metaterm is pure if it is built using only the five productions
on the left-hand side of the grammar. Terms of 𝜆F are identified
with pure metaterms of 𝜆F
𝔪. A metaterm is good if it is pure and
→𝛽-normalizing.
Syntactical differences between 𝜆F
𝔪and 𝜆→
𝔪. The relationship be-

𝔪and 𝜆F is analogous to that of 𝜆→
𝔪and 𝜆→. We mention
some of the differences between 𝜆F

and applications 𝑀𝐴, which abstract over types. Second, the syntax
of the metacalculus for minimal logic 𝜆→
𝔪of the previous section in-
cluded only generators and verifiers of the forms ✠a and ⟨a⟩, where
a was an atomic type. Generators and verifiers for more complex

𝔪includes generators and verifiers for arbitrary

cause in 𝜆F
𝔪type-variables may become instantiated dynamically,
for example (Λ𝑎. ✠𝑎) 𝐴→✠𝐴. Third, 𝜆F
𝔪includes a constructor

constant type, in the scope of 𝑀. This construct is used to verify
universal quantifiers.

Rules 1 and 2 are 𝛽-reduction rules for term-abstraction and type-
abstraction. Rule 3 is the same as in 𝜆→
𝔪. Rule 4 is the same as in
𝜆→
𝔪, but only applies when a is an eigenvariable, not a type-variable.
Rules 5 and 6 implement verification and generation of the impli-
cation, with the same behavior as in minimal logic (Thm. 3.1) but
through reduction rules instead of definitional equations. Rules 7
and 8 are the most interesting ones, as they implement verifica-
tion and generation of the second-order universal quantifier, which
is the new connective. Rule 7 states that the generic realizer for
∀𝑎. 𝐴is a type-abstraction that receives a type 𝑎and returns the
generic realizer ✠𝐴, where 𝐴may depend on 𝑎. Rule 8 states that
to verify whether 𝑀is a realizer for 𝐴one has to check whether
the application 𝑀a is a realizer for ⟨𝐴{𝑎:= a}⟩, where a is a fresh
eigenvariable. Eigenvariables represent “symbolic” or “constant”
types. Finally, rule 9 removes the fresh eigenvariable introduction
when it becomes unnecessary.

order encoding of conjunction, 𝐴∧𝐵:= ∀𝑐. (𝐴⇒𝐵⇒𝑐) ⇒𝑐.

Rewriting properties of 𝜆F
𝔪. Unlike weak head reduction in the
simply typed case, weak head reduction is not deterministic in the
second-order case. For example, if 𝑀→𝑤𝑀′ then ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩𝑀→𝑤
⟨𝐵⟩(𝑀✠𝐴) and ⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩𝑀→𝑤⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩𝑀′. However, it is easy
to show that weak head reduction in 𝜆F
𝔪enjoys a strong form of
confluence (see Sec. A.1 for details):
Proposition 4.2. Weak head reduction →𝑤is subcommutative.
Regarding confluence of reduction, note that the fourth rewrit-
ing rule of 𝜆F
𝔪is not left-linear. However, because eigenvariables
cannot be instantiated, confluence can still be established following
standard techniques: defining a notion of simultaneous reduction
(▷) such that →⊆▷⊆↠and such that ▷satisfies the diamond
property. (A detailed proof is provided in Sec. B.2):
Proposition 4.3. Reduction in 𝜆F
𝔪is confluent.
As in the case of 𝜆→
𝔪, reductions to ★in 𝜆F
𝔪can be standardized
to weak head reductions. We omit the proof of standardization due
to lack of space, but see Sec. B.3 for details. The proof relies on
an intersection type system [11], in particular with non-idempotent
intersection [9, 21]. These type systems can be used to characterize
dynamic properties of programs such as head, weak, and strong
normalization [8], and it is well known they can be used to prove
standardization results [28].
Proposition 4.4 (Standardization). If 𝑀↠★then 𝑀↠𝑤★.
Proof. See Sec. B.3 for the proof. The idea is to propose a type
system T based on non-idempotent intersection types, with two key
properties. First, weak head subject reduction ensures that if
𝑀→𝑤𝑀′ and 𝑀is typable in T then 𝑀′ is typable in T, where
moreover the size of the derivation tree for 𝑀′ is strictly smaller
than the one for 𝑀. Second, subject expansion ensures that if
𝑀→𝑀′ and 𝑀′ is typable in T then 𝑀is typable in T.
Suppose that 𝑀↠★. The constant ★is trivially typable in T, so
by subject expansion 𝑀is also typable. Let 𝑀→𝑤𝑀1 →𝑤𝑀2 . . .
be a maximal sequence of weak head reduction steps. The sequence
must be finite because weak head subject reduction ensures that
the size of the typing derivation decreases. So there exists 𝑛such
that 𝑀↠𝑤𝑀𝑛and 𝑀𝑛is a →𝑤-normal form. Since 𝑀↠𝑀𝑛and
𝑀↠★, by confluence (Thm. 4.3) one concludes that 𝑀𝑛= ★.
□
4.1
Soundness of 𝜆F with respect to 𝜆F
𝔪
We start by mentioning that the correctness lemma is easy to
prove for 𝜆F
𝔪by induction on the size of 𝐴(see Thm. B.14 in the
appendix for the proof):
Lemma 4.5 (Correctness). If 𝐴has no free type-variables then
⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴↠𝑤★.
Note that correctness only holds for types without free type-
variables. For example, ⟨𝑎⟩✠𝑎is irreducible (since 𝑎is a type-
variable), while ⟨a⟩✠a →★holds for an eigenvariable a.
Unfortunately, it is not clear how to extend the syntactic proof
of soundness for minimal logic (Thm. 3.11) to second-order logic.
The kind of difficulties that arise are quite typical in impredicative
settings. In particular, the proof of universality for the metacalcu-
lus for minimal logic 𝜆→
𝔪(Thm. 3.10) proceeded by induction using
a well-founded measure that depended on the size of types. This
measure cannot be easily adapted to the second-order metacalculus
𝜆F
𝔪, because contracting a redex (∀𝑎. 𝑀) 𝐴results in a metaterm
𝑀{𝑎:= 𝐴} in which, for example, a generator ✠𝐵occurring inside
𝑀becomes a generator ✠𝐵{𝑎:=𝐴} for a potentially larger type.
Verification candidates. To prove soundness, we use a notion
we call verification candidates, inspired by reducibility candidates
in Girard’s proof of strong normalization for System F [22]. A
verification candidate is a set of metaterms enjoying certain closure
properties. The proof technique essentially works by mapping each
type 𝐴to a verification candidate J𝐴K. A first key lemma ensures
that any metaterm 𝑀∈J𝐴K is accepted by the verifier for 𝐴, i.e. that
⟨𝐴⟩𝑀↠𝑤★. A second key lemma ensures that each typable term of
type 𝐴belongs to J𝐴K. As usual in these cases, the statements of all
results have to be generalized in order to consider the interpretation
J𝐴K𝜂of a type𝐴under an appropriate mapping𝜂, called a candidate-
assignment, that provides an interpretation for free type-variables.
Definition 4.6 (Verification candidates). Let 𝐴be a type. A set of
metaterms 𝑋is an 𝐴-verification candidate if the three following
conditions hold: (1) ✠𝐴∈𝑋, (2) if 𝑀∈𝑋, then ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀↠𝑤★,
(3) if 𝑀, 𝑀′ are metaterms such that 𝑀→𝑤𝑀′, then 𝑀∈𝑋if and
only if 𝑀′ ∈𝑋.
We write VC𝐴for the set of all 𝐴-verification candidates.
Interpreting types as verification candidates. A type-substitution
is a function 𝜎mapping each type-variable 𝑎to a type 𝜎(𝑎). A
candidate-assignment is a function 𝜂mapping each type-variable
𝑎to a pair (𝐴,𝑋), where 𝐴is a closed type, i.e. a type without
free type-variables, and 𝑋is an 𝐴-verification candidate. We write
𝜂[𝑎↦→(𝐴,𝑋)] for the candidate-assignment such that (𝜂[𝑎↦→
(𝐴,𝑋)])(𝑎) = (𝐴,𝑋) and (𝜂[𝑎↦→(𝐴,𝑋)])(𝑏) = 𝜂(𝑏) for any type-
variable 𝑏other than 𝑎. For each candidate-assignment 𝜂, we define
its first projection as the type-substitution ˜𝜂such that ˜𝜂(𝑎) = 𝐴
whenever 𝜂(𝑎) = (𝐴,𝑋). By abuse of notation, sometimes we write
𝜂for the first projection of the candidate-assignment 𝜂instead of ˜𝜂
(e.g. 𝐴𝜂stands for 𝐴˜𝜂).
Definition 4.7 (Candidate interpretation of types). Let 𝐴be a type
and 𝜂be a candidate-assignment. We define a set of metaterms
J𝐴K𝜂by recursion on 𝐴as follows:
1. J𝑎K𝜂:= 𝑋if 𝜂(𝑎) = (𝐴,𝑋).
2. JaK𝜂is the set of metaterms 𝑀such that 𝑀↠𝑤✠a.

Below, we mention three key properties of the interpretation of
types. We have omitted the detailed proofs but they can be found
in Sec. B.4. First, the interpretation of a type 𝐴always yields an
𝐴-verification candidate. More precisely, the following adequacy
lemma holds (see Thm. B.15 for the proof):
Lemma 4.8 (Adeqacy for types). J𝐴K𝜂∈VC𝐴𝜂
Second, the two lemmas below are typical in reducibility inter-

first lemma expresses an irrelevance property, and the second one
expresses a substitution property. (See Thm. B.16 and Thm. B.17 for
the proofs):
Lemma 4.9. If 𝜂1 and 𝜂2 agree on the free type-variables of 𝐴, then
J𝐴K𝜂1 = J𝐴K𝜂2.
Lemma 4.10. J𝐴{𝑎:= 𝐵}K𝜂= J𝐴K𝜂[𝑎↦→(𝐵𝜂, J𝐵K𝜂)]
Proving soundness through verification candidates. Before stating
and proving the main result, we require a few more definitions. A
substitution is a function 𝛾mapping each variable to a metaterm.
The generative substitution for an environment Γ is written Γ and
defined as the substitution such that Γ(𝑥) = ✠𝐴holds for every
𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ, and Γ(𝑥) = 𝑥holds for every 𝑥∉dom(Γ). By abuse of
notation, sometimes we write just Γ for the generative substitution,
rather than Γ. If 𝜂is a candidate-assignment, Γ is an environment,
and 𝛾is a substitution, we say that 𝛾is compatible with Γ under
𝜂, written Γ ⊨𝜂𝛾, if for every variable 𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ we have that
𝑥𝛾∈J𝐴K𝜂.
The main lemma to prove soundness roughly states that all terms
of type 𝐴are elements of the 𝐴-verification candidate given by J𝐴K.
The precise statement is more general, to account for the presence
of potentially free variables and type-variables:
Lemma 4.11 (Adeqacy for terms). Let Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴and Γ ⊨𝜂𝛾.
Then 𝑡𝜂𝛾∈J𝐴K𝜂.
Proof. By induction on the derivation of the typing judgment
Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴. See Thm. B.18 for the proof.
□
Theorem 4.12 (Soundness). Let Γ,𝑡,𝐴be such that they have no
free type-variables7. If Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴then ⟨𝐴⟩𝑡Γ ↠★.
Proof. Let a be a fixed eigenvariable, and let JaK be the set of
metaterms 𝑀such that 𝑀↠𝑤✠a. Observe that 𝜂(𝑎) = (a, JaK) is
a candidate-assignment. Note that Γ ⊨𝜂Γ, where Γ is the generative
substitution for Γ, because if 𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ then ⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴↠★by correct-
ness (Thm. 4.5). Then by Thm. 4.11 we have 𝑡𝜂Γ ∈J𝐴K𝜂. Finally, by
Thm. 4.8 J𝐴K𝜂is a 𝐴𝜂-verification candidate, so ⟨𝐴𝜂⟩𝑡𝜂Γ ↠★. Since
𝐴and 𝑡have no free type-variables this means that ⟨𝐴⟩𝑡Γ ↠★.
□

Theorem 4.14 (Completeness). If ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀Γ ↠★and 𝑀is good,

5
Extension to Higher-Order Logic
In this section, we discuss how to extend the epistemic realizability
interpretations of the previous sections to higher-order logic. Re-
call that higher-order logic generalizes systems such as first and
second-order logic by allowing universal quantifiers to bind vari-
ables of arbitrarily high order. Type-variables may represent not
only elements of the universe of discourse, but also predicates on
these elements, predicates on these predicates, and so on. Due to
its expressivity, it has been used as the basis of functional program-
ming languages such as Haskell, and proof assistants such as HOL
Light or Isabelle/HOL. Many formalizations of higher-order logic
can be found in the literature, starting with Church’s simple theory
of types [10]. Our work is based on Girard’s System F𝜔[22], one of
the vertices of Barendregt’s 𝜆-cube [4]. System F𝜔is a two-layered
typed 𝜆-calculus with three sorts of expressions: kinds, types, and
terms.
The main results in this section are again soundness (Thm. 5.2)
and completeness (Thm. 5.3).
The higher-order 𝜆-calculus (𝜆F𝜔). We recall the typed 𝜆-calculus
corresponding to higher-order logic, namely Girard’s System F𝜔[22,
44]. The set of kinds is given by the following grammar, including
base kinds (𝜅,𝜅′, . . .) and a constant Prop:
Kinds
𝐾, 𝐽, . . . ::= 𝜅| Prop | 𝐾→𝐽
As in the second-order case, we assume given disjoint sets of type-
variables (𝑎,𝑏, . . .) and eigenvariables (a, b, . . .). The set of types is
given by the grammar:
Types
𝐴, 𝐵, . . . ::= 𝑎| a | 𝜆𝑎𝐾. 𝐴| 𝐴𝐵| 𝐴⇒𝐵| ∀𝑎𝐾. 𝐴
A kind-environment (𝜉, 𝜉′, . . .) is a finite set of assignments 𝑎1 :
𝐾1, . . . ,𝑎𝑛: 𝐾𝑛, a1 : 𝐾𝑛+1, . . . , a𝑚: 𝐾𝑛+𝑚, mapping type-variables
and eigenvariables to kinds. Kinding judgments are of the form
𝜉⊢𝐴: 𝐾, meaning that the type 𝐴is well-kinded of kind 𝐾under
the kind-environment 𝜉. The typing rules are the expected ones. In
particular, 𝜉⊢𝐴⇒𝐵: Prop holds if 𝜉⊢𝐴: Prop and 𝜉⊢𝐵: Prop
hold; and 𝜉⊢∀𝑎𝐾. 𝐴: Prop holds if 𝜉,𝑎: 𝐾⊢𝐴: Prop holds. We
define 𝛽-convertibility as the least congruence generated by the
usual 𝛽-reduction rule:
(𝜆𝑎𝐾. 𝐴) 𝐵→𝛽𝐴{𝑎:= 𝐵}
Types of kind Prop are called propositions. For example, the type
∀𝑃𝜅→Prop. ∀𝑓𝜅→𝜅. ∀𝑎𝜅. (𝑃𝑎⇒𝑃(𝑓𝑎)) is a proposition. Note that
type-variables bound by universal quantifiers may play the role of
predicate symbols (e.g. 𝑃) or function symbols (e.g. 𝑓).
Terms and typing judgments. Given a denumerable set of vari-
ables (𝑥,𝑦, . . .), the set of terms is given by the grammar:
Terms
𝑡,𝑠, . . . ::= 𝑥| 𝜆𝑥.𝑡| 𝑡𝑠| Λ𝑎𝐾.𝑡| 𝑡𝐴
Note that the grammar of terms is similar to the one for the second-
order case, with the difference that type-variables bound by type-
abstractions are decorated with their kind. We write 𝜆𝑥.𝑡instead
of 𝜆𝑥.𝑡to avoid confusion with the abstraction at the level of types.
An environment (Γ, Γ′, . . .) is a finite set of {𝑥1 : 𝐴1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛: 𝐴𝑛}
that maps distinct variables to types. We introduce two further
forms of judgment: (1) 𝜉⊢Γ env, meaning that the environment Γ
is well-formed under the kind-environment 𝜉(i.e. it maps variables
to propositions), and (2) 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴, meaning that the term 𝑡is a
proof of the proposition 𝐴under the environments 𝜉and Γ. Valid
judgments are given by:

The notions of pure and good metaterms are defined as for 𝜆F

The higher-order metacalculus 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪is similar to second-order
metacalculus 𝜆F
𝔪, except for the fact that Λ𝑎𝐾. 𝑀and𝜈a𝐾. 𝑀include
a kind decoration. This was not necessary in 𝜆F

Global kind-assignment. Kinds and types of expressions in 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪

to the following well-established convention [20, 22]. We assume
that there is a global kind-assignment Ξ, i.e. a function mapping each
type-variable 𝑎and each eigenvariable a to its kind. We assume
that the global kind-assignment has “enough” variables of each
kind, i.e. for each kind 𝐾there are denumerably infinite sets of
type-variables and eigenvariables of kind 𝐾. Whenever we have
an expression like Λ𝑎𝐾. 𝑀that binds a type-variable of kind 𝐾,
and in case we need to reason about the body 𝑀, we assume (by
𝛼-renaming if necessary) that 𝑎has been chosen in such a way that
Ξ(𝑎) = 𝐾, and similarly for eigenvariables. We write 𝜉⊆Ξ if the
kind-environment 𝜉is compatible with Ξ, i.e. it assigns the same
kinds as Ξ to type-variables and eigenvariables. We write Ξ ⊢𝐴: 𝐾
to mean that 𝜉⊢𝐴: 𝐾holds for some 𝜉⊆Ξ. Similarly, we may
write Ξ ⊢Γ env to mean that 𝜉⊢Γ env holds, and Ξ; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴to
mean that 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴holds, for some 𝜉⊆Ξ

Well-kindedness for pre-metaterms. The 𝜆→
𝔪and 𝜆F
𝔪calculi of
previous sections were completely untyped. In contrast, in 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪we
shall impose an invariant enforcing that pre-metaterms be well-
kinded. Specifically, well-kindedness imposes the restriction that,
in all occurrences of ✠𝐴and ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀the type 𝐴is a proposition, and
that in each type-application 𝑀𝐴the type 𝐴is well-kinded. For
example, 𝜈aProp. ✠a is well-kinded, while 𝜈a𝐾→Prop. ✠a (in which
✠a generates a “realizer” for a type that is not a proposition) is not
well-kinded. The metacalculus is still untyped, in that metaterms
are not required to be well-typed; for example, 𝑥𝑥is well-kinded.
Formally, we say that a pre-metaterm 𝑀is well-kinded if the
predicate wk(𝑀) holds, according to the following inductive rules:

We work with metaterms by writing pre-metaterms, treating them
implicitly modulo 𝜏-equivalence. As usual in other settings (e.g.
higher-order rewriting systems), we say that a type-variable or
eigenvariable occurs free in a metaterm 𝑀if it occurs free in
every representative. From now on, expressions 𝑀, 𝑁, . . . denote
metaterms rather than pre-metaterms. We shall not explicitly state
lemmas showing that constructions can be lifted from pre-metaterms
to metaterms, but these lifting properties are easy due to the stan-
dard fact that well-kinded types have unique 𝛽-normal forms.

The rewriting rules are almost exactly the same as to those of the
second-order metacalculus 𝜆F
𝔪. There are two noteworthy differ-
ences. First, rule 2 requires that the redex is well-kinded to proceed,
i.e. that the actual argument 𝐴has the same kind as the formal
parameter 𝑎. For example, the step (Λ𝑎Prop. ✠𝑎⇒𝑎) b →✠b⇒b is
allowed if and only if b : Prop ∈Ξ. This side condition allows to
show that reduction preserves well-kindedness.
Second, note that if 𝐴is a well-kinded proposition without free
type-variables, it must be exactly of one of three possible forms
up to 𝛽-convertibility: an applied eigenvariable (a𝐴1 . . . 𝐴𝑛), an
implication (𝐵⇒𝐶), or a universal quantification (∀𝑎𝐾. 𝐵). The
reduction rule 4 treats the case for a𝐴1 . . . 𝐴𝑛, written a ®𝐴in the rule.
Contrast this with the rule for 𝜆F
𝔪, which was just ⟨a⟩✠a →★. In
the second-order case, all eigenvariables were morally propositions
(of kind Prop), and could not be applied, while in 𝜆F𝜔they can also
play the role of predicate symbols (of kind 𝐾1 →. . . →𝐾𝑛→Prop).
Example 5.1 (Symmetry of Leibniz equality). Let 𝐴, 𝐵be types of
kind 𝐾, and let Leibniz equality be defined by its usual encoding
(𝐴=𝐾𝐵) := ∀𝑃𝐾→Prop. (𝑃𝐴⇒𝑃𝐵). Let 𝑄𝑃:= 𝜆𝑐𝐾. (𝑃𝑐⇒𝑃𝐴).
Then the following reduction shows that symmetry is realizable:
⟨(𝐴=𝐾𝐵) ⇒(𝐵=𝐾𝐴)⟩(𝜆𝑒. Λ𝑃𝐾→Prop. 𝜆𝑥.𝑒𝑄𝑃(𝜆𝑦.𝑦) 𝑥)
↠⟨𝐵=𝐾𝐴⟩(Λ𝑃𝐾→Prop. 𝜆𝑥. ✠𝐴=𝐾𝐵𝑄𝑃(𝜆𝑦.𝑦) 𝑥)
↠𝜈p𝐾→Prop. ⟨p𝐵⇒p𝐴⟩(𝜆𝑥. ✠𝐴=𝐾𝐵𝑄p (𝜆𝑦.𝑦) 𝑥)

≡𝜈p𝐾→Prop. ⟨p𝐴⟩(✠(p𝐴⇒p𝐴)⇒p𝐵⇒p𝐴(𝜆𝑦.𝑦) ✠p𝐵) ↠★
Soundness and completeness of 𝜆F𝜔with respect to 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪. The rest of
the development follows closely what we have done in the second-
order case (Sec. 4). In particular, the notion of generative substitution
Γ is defined as before, and 𝑀Γ denotes 𝑀Γ. The proofs of soundness
and completeness are extensions of those for the second-order
case (Thm. 4.12 and Thm. 4.14). The proof of completeness is a
straightforward extension, while the proof of soundness requires to
parameterize the interpretation with respect to an indexed family of
verification candidates, using types as indices, to be able to interpret
higher-kinded types. This follows well-established techniques [20,
22]. Details for the proof of soundness can be found in Sec. C.1.
Theorem 5.2 (Soundness). Let Γ,𝑡,𝐴be such that they have no
free type-variables. If Ξ; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴then ⟨𝐴⟩𝑡Γ ↠★.
Theorem 5.3 (Completeness). Let Ξ ⊢𝐴: Prop and wk(𝑀) and
Ξ ⊢Γ env. If ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀Γ ↠★and 𝑀is good, then its 𝛽-normal form is a

## Page 12


Verifiers and Generators: Epistemic Semantics for Intuitionistic Logic
P. Barenbaum
6
Conclusion
This paper formulates epistemic realizability interpretations for
minimal, second, and higher-order logic, showing that each of
the three systems is sound (Thms. 3.11, 4.12 and 5.2) and complete
(Thms. 3.14, 4.14 and 5.3).
Related work. Our main motivation is philosophical: to explain
the meaning of propositions from a verificationist perspective,
but imposing the condition that the semantics is “epistemic”, i.e.
that recognizing evidence for a proposition is semi-decidable. Sim-
ilar concerns underly several foundational programs, including
Kreisel’s theory of constructions [30], Prawitz’s proof-theoretic
semantics [43], Dummett’s anti-realist theory of meaning [15], and
Martin-Löf’s meaning explanations [33]. In those works, the mean-
ing of a proposition is defined by the conditions for making a judg-
ment that asserts it, while in our case the meaning of a proposition
is defined compositionally, without referring to the notion of judg-
ment. For example, the meaning of 𝐴⇒𝐵is a pair of programs
(⟨𝐴⇒𝐵⟩, ✠𝐴⇒𝐵) which are defined in terms of the meanings
of 𝐴and 𝐵, i.e. in terms of the pairs of programs (⟨𝐴⟩, ✠𝐴) and
(⟨𝐵⟩, ✠𝐵), without making reference to a judgment like 𝐴⊢𝐵.
Our motivation is perhaps closest to that of Girard’s Ludics [16,
17, 23, 24, 51], in which the meaning of propositions is given by the
interaction between proofs and counter-proofs (related to verifiers)
which may include daimons (related to generators).
The notions of correctness and universality in this paper resemble
the notion of harmony of introduction and elimination rules [15,
55], and specifically the conditions of local soundness and local
completeness [13, 42].
The motivation behind realizability interpretations is to extract
computational content from proofs. Although some realizability
interpretations extract 𝜆-terms from proofs (e.g. Kreisel’s), as far
as we know, none of these intepretations express the verification
procedure as a program. An active field of research is that of Krivine
realizability [31], as a means to extract computational content from
classical and set theoretical reasoning principles [14, 18, 26, 34, 35].
Limitations. Our completeness theorems require that the realizer
is “good”: both pure and 𝛽-normalizing. We conjecture that they can
be strengthened to avoid relying on 𝛽-normalization. One strategy
could be to show that ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀Γ ↠★implies that 𝑀is 𝛽-normalizing,
through a non-idempotent intersection type system (cf. Thm. 4.4).
It is unclear how to extend the semantics to more expressive sys-
tems, e.g. allowing dependent elimination on datatypes. Inductive
and coinductive datatypes can be encoded in type theories with
impredicative universes [1, 7]. While these encodings can be repro-
duced in higher-order logic, the resulting datatypes only support
non-dependent elimination.
Potential application: “typeless types”. One interesting aspect is
that verifiers and generators can be implemented directly by the
user in an untyped programming language that supports features
such as first-class functions. For example, the operators of Sec. 4
can be easily implemented in a language such as Python or a Lisp
dialect. The verifier ⟨𝐴⟩can then be used to dynamically check
whether an untyped program 𝑀behaves as a term of type𝐴, without
inspecting the source code8. This could be a way to integrate static
and dynamic type systems, as in gradual typing [48].
In a similar spirit, epistemic realizability could potentially be-
come the basis of proof assistants in which proof checking is imple-
mented not by statically analyzing the proofs, but by dynamically
running the verifier. These tools would not require type annotations
on the source proofs, and they would not need to perform complex
operations such as elaboration or higher-order unification9.
Potential as a foundation for ultrafinitism. Ultrafinitism is a branch
of constructivism concerned with feasibility [36, 40, 46, 53]. An ul-
trafinitist may deny that an expression like 21000 denotes a natural
number, on the grounds that computational resources are bounded:
a computation exceeding our available time is effectively indistin-
guishable from a non-terminating one. The question of providing
logical foundations for ultrafinitism has not been definitely settled,
as attested by some recent and ongoing works [19, 27, 32].
The epistemic realizability semantics proposed in this paper
could perhaps provide the underlying semantics for an ultrafinitis-
tic logic. For example, taking the usual second-order encoding of
natural numbers, N := ∀𝑎. (𝑎⇒(𝑎⇒𝑎) ⇒𝑎) and the usual defi-
nition of exponentiation in this setting, it is feasible to check that the
function 𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛is a realizer for N ⇒N, as it amounts to a “short” cal-
culation ⟨N ⇒N⟩(𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛) ↠⟨N⟩(2✠N) ↠★. On the other hand,
it is unfeasible to check that (𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛) 1000 is a realizer for N, as it
would amount to a calculation ⟨N⟩((𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛) 1000) ↠⟨N⟩21000 ↠★
that exceeds our computational resources10. This seems consistent
with the positions of some ultrafinitists, including Sazonov’s pro-
posal that only normal (cut-free) proofs should be allowed [46], and
Zeilberger’s view that general theorems about “all” integers are
actually statements about symbolic integers [54].
Future work. It would be interesting to provide a semantics for
positive connectives directly, without resorting to their second-
order encodings. For example, the generator ✠𝐴∨𝐵should behave
non-deterministically as either inj1(✠𝐴) or inj2(✠𝐵).
Epistemic realizability relates typability (⊢𝑡: 𝐴) and success-
ful termination (⟨𝐴⟩𝑡↠★). This may provide a new strategy to
connect simple types and intersection types, which is the topic of
recent work [41].
As part of ongoing work, we attempt to extend the semantics
to classical propositional logic, using a variant of Parigot’s 𝜆𝜇-
calculus [39]. We expect that in linear and classical calculi based
on sequent calculus, such as ¯𝜆𝜇˜𝜇[12], the duality between verifiers
and generators should become more apparent, with the verifier for
𝐴corresponding to the generator for ¬𝐴.
An alternative way to provide a epistemic semantics for minimal
logic is through an idea, due to Scott [47], that each proposition 𝐴
can be interpreted as an idempotent operator J𝐴K and each typable
term ⊢𝑡: 𝐴as a fixed point J𝑡K of the operator J𝐴K. The problem
is whether this idea can be extended to second and higher-order
logic.
8This assumes, of course, that 𝑀has no side-effects.
9Error reporting, however, could be challenging.
10Note that our soundness and completeness results entail that the typing rule for
application is valid, so from ⟨N ⇒N⟩(𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛) ↠★and ⟨N⟩1000 ↠★one may
conclude that ⟨N⟩((𝜆𝑛. 2𝑛) 1000) ↠★. A radical ultrafinitist would likely reject the
validity of the meta-theoretical reasoning used to justify this.

Lemma B.4 (Reflexivity). 𝑀▷𝑀
Proof. Straightforward by induction on 𝑀.
□
Lemma B.5 (Substitution). If 𝑀▷𝑀′ and 𝑁▷𝑁′ then 𝑀{𝑥:=
𝑁} ▷𝑀′{𝑥:= 𝑁′}.
Proof. Straightforward by induction on the derivation of 𝑀▷
𝑀′, resorting to reflexivity (Thm. B.4) in the var case.
□
Lemma B.6 (Type substitution). If 𝑀▷𝑀′ then 𝑀{𝑎:= 𝐴} ▷
𝑀′{𝑎:= 𝐴}.
Proof. Straightforward by induction on the derivation of 𝑀▷
𝑀′. Note that in the v-eig case we have that 𝑀= ⟨a⟩✠a ▷★= 𝑀′
where a is a eigenvariable so a ≠𝑎.

Lemma B.7 (Tait–Martin-Löf techniqe).
1. →⊆▷
2. ▷⊆↠
3. Diamond property: ◁▷⊆▷◁
Proof. For the first item, suppose that 𝑀→𝑁. It is straight-
forward to show that 𝑀▷𝑁by induction on 𝑀and resorting to
reflexivity (Thm. B.4). For the second item, suppose that 𝑀▷𝑁.
It is straightforward to show that 𝑀↠𝑁by induction on the
derivation of 𝑀▷𝑁.
For the third item, suppose that 𝑀1 ▷𝑀2 and 𝑀1 ▷𝑀3 and let us
show that there exists a term 𝑀4 such that 𝑀2 ▷𝑀4 and 𝑀3 ▷𝑀4.

If 𝑀1 is a generator (𝑀1 = ✠𝐴), note first that if both steps are
derived using the same rule, it is immediate to conclude because
𝑀2 = 𝑀3 and it suffices to take 𝑀4 := 𝑀2 and resort to reflex-
ivity (Thm. B.4). Moreover, note that it is not possible to derive
one step using the g-imp rule and the other one using the g-all
rule, because we would simultaneously have 𝐴= (𝐵⇒𝐶) and
𝐴= (∀𝑎. 𝐷), which is impossible. The remaining cases are when
one step is derived using the g-refl rule and the other one is de-
rived using either the g-imp or the g-all rule. These cases are
immediate because if 𝑀1 ▷𝑀2 is derived using the g-refl rule
then 𝑀2 = 𝑀1, and it suffices to take 𝑀4 := 𝑀3.
If 𝑀1 is a verifier (𝑀1 = ⟨𝐴⟩𝑁1), note first that if both steps are
derived using the same rule it is immediate to conclude by resorting
to the i.h.. Moreover, note that the rules v-eig, v-imp, and v-all
are mutually exclusive, because 𝐴cannot be simultaneously of two
shapes (e.g. an eigenvariable and an implication). The remaining
cases are when one step is derived using the v-cong rule and the

rule:

𝑀1 ▷𝑀2 is derived from the v-cong rule. Then necessarily
𝑀2 = ⟨a⟩✠a, and the situation is:

B.3
Second-Order: Standardization (Thm. 4.4)
We use a non-idempotent intersection type system T to prove
standardization (Thm. 4.4).
The sets of linear-types and linear-multitypes of T are given
mutually inductively by the grammar:
Linear-types

Linear-multitypes are finite multisets of linear-types. The notation
[T𝑖]𝑖∈𝐼stands for a multiset of elements indexed by a finite set 𝐼,
that sometimes we may also write more explicitly as [T1, . . . , T𝑛].
The size of a linear-multitype M is written |M| and defined as its
cardinality as a multiset (i.e. number of elements, counting their
multiplicities). We write M + N for the union of linear-multitypes.
A linear-environment is a function E mapping each variable to a
linear-multitype. We assume that linear-environments have finite
support, i.e. that E(𝑥) ≠[ ] for only finitely many variables. If E, F
are linear-environments, we write E + F for the linear-environment
such that (E + F)(𝑥) = E(𝑥) + F(𝑥) for each variable 𝑥.
We define two forms of typing judgments, E ⊢𝑀: T and E ⊩
𝑀: M. Valid judgments in system T are defined inductively by the
following rules:

Derivation trees (or just derivations) in this system are ranged over
by Φ, Ψ, . . .. We write Φ ▷E ⊢𝑀: T to mean that Φ is a derivation
tree with conclusion E ⊢𝑀: T. The size of a derivation Φ is written
|Φ| and defined as the number of inference rules in Φ, without
counting instances of the Lmulti rule.
Lemma B.9 (Weighted substitution lemma). If Φ ▷E,𝑥: M ⊢
𝑀: T and Ψ ▷F ⊩𝑁: M where 𝑥∉fv(𝑁), then there exists a
derivation Φ′ such that Φ′ ▷E + F ⊢𝑀{𝑥:= 𝑁} : T and |Φ′| =
|Φ| −|M| + |Ψ|.
Proof. By induction on Φ:
Case Lvar: Let Φ ▷𝑦: [T] ⊢𝑦: T. We consider two subcases,
depending on whether 𝑦= 𝑥or not:
1. If 𝑀= 𝑦= 𝑥, the derivation Φ is of the form Φ ▷𝑥: [T] ⊢𝑥:
T, so E = ∅and M = [T]. Moreover, the derivation Ψ is of
the form Ψ ▷F ⊩𝑁: [T] and it must be derived by Lmulti
from a single premise Ψ′ ▷F ⊢𝑁: T. Our goal is to construct
a derivation with conclusion E + F ⊢𝑀{𝑥:= 𝑁} : T, that
is to say, F ⊢𝑁: T. It suffices to take Φ′ := Ψ′. Note that
|Φ| = 1 and |M| = 1, so |Φ′| = |Ψ′| = |Ψ| = |Φ| −|M| + |Ψ|.
2. If 𝑀= 𝑦≠𝑥, the derivation Φ is of the form Φ ▷𝑦: [T] ⊢
𝑦: T, so E = (𝑦: [T]) and M = [ ]. Moreover, the derivation
Ψ is of the form Ψ ▷F ⊩𝑁: [ ] and it must be derived
by Lmulti with the empty set of premises, so in particular
F = ∅and |Ψ| = 0. Our goal is to construct a derivation
with conclusion E + F ⊢𝑀{𝑥:= 𝑁} : T, that is to say,
𝑦: [T] ⊢𝑦: T. It suffices to take Φ′ := Φ. Note that |M| = 0,
so |Φ′| = |Φ| = |Φ| −|M| + |Ψ|.
Case Llam: Let Φ be of the form:

Lemma B.11 (Anti-substitution lemma). If Φ ▷E ⊢𝑀{𝑥:=
𝑁} : T, then there exist linear-environments E0, F and a linear-
multitype M such that E = E0 + F and Φ0 ▷E0,𝑥: M ⊢𝑀: T
and Ψ ▷F ⊩𝑁: M.
Proof. By induction on Φ. We start by considering the case in
which 𝑀= 𝑥. In such case, we have by hypothesis Φ ▷E ⊢𝑁: T.
So it suffices to take E0 := ∅and F := E and M := [T], with the
derivation Φ0 ▷𝑥: [T] ⊢𝑥: T constructed applying Lvar, and
Ψ ▷E ⊩𝑁: [T] constructed applying Lmulti, with Φ as the single
premise.
Now, in the remainder of the proof, we may assume that 𝑀≠𝑥.
We proceed by case analysis on the last rule applied to construct
Φ. The proof in each case is similar to the weighted substitution
lemma (Thm. B.9), but reasoning backwards. For example, in the
case of rule L?, we have that 𝑀{𝑥:= 𝑁} is of the form 𝑃?𝑄, and
since 𝑀≠𝑥we have that 𝑀= 𝑃0?𝑄0 with 𝑃0{𝑥:= 𝑁} = 𝑃and
𝑄0{𝑥:= 𝑁} = 𝑄. The derivation Φ is of the form:
Φ1
E1 ⊢𝑃0{𝑥:= 𝑁} : ★
Φ2
E1 ⊢𝑄0{𝑥:= 𝑁} : T
E1 + E2 ⊢𝑃0{𝑥:= 𝑁}?𝑄0{𝑥:= 𝑁} : T
L?
where E = E1 + E2. By i.h. on Φ1 we have that there exist E10, F1, M1
such that E1 = E10 + F1 and there exist derivations Φ10 ▷E10,𝑥:
M1 ⊢𝑃0 : ★and Ψ1 ▷F1 ⊩𝑁: M1. Similarly, by i.h. on Φ2 we have
that there exist E20, F2, M2 such that E2 = E20 + F2 and there exist
derivations Φ20 ▷E20,𝑥: M2 ⊢𝑄0 : T and Ψ2 ▷F2 ⊩𝑁: M2.
To conclude, take E0 := E10 + E20 and F := F1 + F2 and M :=
M1 + M2. Note that E = E1 + E2 = E10 + F1 + E20 + F2 = E0 + F.
Applying L? on the derivations Φ10 and Φ20 we obtain a derivation
Φ0▷E10+E20,𝑥: M1+M2 ⊢𝑃0?𝑄0 : T, that is, Φ0▷E0,𝑥: M ⊢𝑀: T.
Moreover, the derivations Ψ1 and Ψ2 must be derived using Lmulti,
so they can be joined to obtain a derivation Ψ1 + Ψ2 ▷F1 + F2 ⊩𝑁:
M1 + M2, that is, Ψ ▷F ⊩𝑁: M.
□
Lemma B.12 (Subject expansion). Let 𝑀→𝑀′. If Φ ▷E ⊢𝑀′ :

by reducing internally to a subterm).

Proposition B.13 (Standardization). If 𝑀↠★then 𝑀↠𝑤★.
Proof. Suppose that 𝑀↠★. Note that ⊢★: ★. Since 𝑀↠★,
by applying subject expansion (Thm. B.12) repeatedly, we obtain a
derivation Φ0 such that Φ0▷⊢𝑀: ★. Consider a maximal, poten-
tially infinite, sequence of weak head reduction steps 𝑀= 𝑀0 →𝑤
𝑀1 →𝑤𝑀2 . . .. Applying weak head subject reduction (Thm. B.10)
repeatedly, we construct a sequence of derivations Φ0, Φ1, Φ2, . . .
such that Φ𝑖▷⊢𝑀𝑖: ★for all 𝑖and such that |Φ𝑖| > |Φ𝑖+1| for all
𝑖. Then the reduction sequence 𝑀= 𝑀0 →𝑤𝑀1 →𝑤𝑀2 . . . must
be finite because otherwise we would have an infinite decreasing
sequence of natural numbers |Φ0| > |Φ1| > |Φ2| > . . .. Let 𝑀𝑛be
the last term of the sequence, which must be a →𝑤-normal form.
Since 𝑀↠★and 𝑀↠𝑀𝑛by confluence (Thm. 4.3) we have that
𝑀𝑛↠★, but 𝑀𝑛is a weak head normal form, so 𝑀𝑛= ★.
□
B.4
Second-Order: Soundness (Thm. 4.12)
The soundness theorem is proved in the main body (Thm. 4.12). In
this section we prove all the required auxiliary lemmas:
• Correctness (Thm. B.14) corresponding to Thm. 4.5 in the
main body.
• Adeqacy for types (Thm. B.15), corresponding to Thm. 4.8
in the main body.
• Irrelevance (Thm. B.16) corresponding to Thm. 4.9 in the
main body.
• Substitution lemma (Thm. B.17) corresponding to Thm. 4.10
in the main body.

⟨𝐵⟩(⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴?✠𝐵)

Lemma B.15 (Adeqacy for types). J𝐴K𝜂∈VC𝐴𝜂
Proof. We proceed by induction on 𝐴.
If 𝐴is a type-variable (𝐴= 𝑎): Let𝜂(𝑎) = (𝐵,𝑋), where we have
that 𝑋∈VC𝐵by definition of 𝜂being a candidate-assignment.
Note that J𝐴K𝜂= J𝑎K𝜂= 𝑋and 𝐴𝜂= 𝑎𝜂= 𝐵, so to check that
J𝐴K𝜂∈VC𝐴𝜂amounts to checking that 𝑋∈VC𝐵, which holds
by hypothesis.
If 𝐴is an eigenvariable (𝐴= a): Note that, by definition, JaK𝜂:=
{𝑀| 𝑀↠𝑤✠a} and a𝜂= a, because eigenvariables are not affected
by substitutions. In order to show that JaK𝜂∈VCa, we check each
of the three conditions defining VCa:

zero reduction steps.
2. Let 𝑀∈JaK𝜂. Then 𝑀↠𝑤✠a, so ⟨a⟩𝑀↠𝑤⟨a⟩✠a →𝑤★.

Lemma B.16 (Irrelevance). If 𝜂1 and 𝜂2 agree on the free vari-

B.5
Second-Order: Completeness (Thm. 4.14)
Lemma B.19 (Characterization of normal forms). A pure
metaterm 𝑀is in 𝛽-normal form if and only if it is a normal term,
according to the grammar of normal forms given in Sec. 4.2.
Proof. Both implications are routine by induction on 𝑀.
□
Remark B.20 (Determinism of input matching). The predicate
𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴⇛𝐴depends functionally on 𝐴and ®𝐼. More precisely,
if 𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴1 ⇛𝐴1 and 𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴2 ⇛𝐴2 hold, then ®𝐴1 = ®𝐴2 and
𝐴1 = 𝐴2.
Remark B.21 (Input matching preserves the length). If 𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴⇛
𝐴holds, then | ®𝐴| = |®𝐼|, i.e. they are lists of the same length.
Lemma B.22 (Input matching of a concatenation). Let 𝐴,𝐴′
be types and ®𝐴be a sequence of types. Moreover, let ®𝐼1, ®𝐼2 be two
sequences of inputs, and let us write ®𝐼1, ®𝐼2 for their concatenation.
Then the following are equivalent:
1. 𝐴/ (®𝐼1, ®𝐼2) : ®𝐴⇛𝐴′
2. There exist sequences of types ®𝐴1, ®𝐴2 and a type 𝐶such that
®𝐴= ®𝐴1, ®𝐴2 and 𝐴/ ®𝐼1 : ®𝐴1 ⇛𝐴′′ and 𝐴′′ / ®𝐼2 : ®𝐴2 ⇛𝐴′.
Proof. By induction on the length of ®𝐼1. We only prove the

⟨a⟩((𝜆𝑥. ⟨𝐵⟩𝑥?✠𝐶) 𝑀®𝐽) ↠⟨a⟩((⟨𝐵⟩𝑀?✠𝐶) ®𝐽)
so by confluence (Thm. 4.3) we have that ⟨a⟩((⟨𝐵⟩𝑀?✠𝐶) ®𝐽) ↠★.
Then we necessarily have that ⟨𝐵⟩𝑀↠★and ⟨a⟩(✠𝐶®𝐽) ↠★.
Since |®𝐼| = 1 + | ®𝐽|, we may resort to the i.h., obtaining that there
exists a sequence of types ®𝐶such that 𝐶/ ®𝐽: ®𝐶⇛a and such that
⟨𝐶𝑖⟩𝐽𝑖↠★holds whenever 1 ≤𝑖≤𝑛−1 and 𝐽𝑖is a metaterm.
To conclude, take ®𝐴:= (𝐵, ®𝐶) where 𝐵is the first element and ®𝐶
is the tail. Observe that 𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴⇛a holds because it means that
(𝐵⇒𝐶) / (𝑀, ®𝐽) : (𝐵, ®𝐶) ⇛a which in turn holds by definition,
because we have that 𝐶/ ®𝐽: ®𝐶⇛a. Moreover, the condition
⟨𝐴𝑖⟩𝐼𝑖↠★holds whenever 1 ≤𝑖≤𝑛and 𝐼𝑖is a metaterm. Indeed,
when 𝑖= 1 this corresponds to the fact that ⟨𝐵⟩𝑀↠★, and when
𝑖> 1 this follows from the i.h..
If 𝐴is a universal quantification, i.e. 𝐴= (∀𝑏. 𝐵): Then we
have that ⟨a⟩✠∀𝑏. 𝐵®𝐼↠★, and we can note that ⟨a⟩✠∀𝑏. 𝐵®𝐼→
⟨a⟩((Λ𝑏. ✠𝐵) ®𝐼) so by confluence (Thm. 4.3) we have that also
⟨a⟩((Λ𝑏. ✠𝐵) ®𝐼) ↠★. We claim that ®𝐼must be of the form ®𝐼= (𝐶, ®𝐽)
where the first element𝐶is a type, and ®𝐽is the tail. Indeed, note that

a contradiction.
Therefore, ®𝐼= (𝐶, ®𝐽) where 𝐶is a type, and we have that

so by confluence (Thm. 4.3) we have that ⟨a⟩(✠𝐵{𝑏:=𝐶} ®𝐽) ↠★.
Since |®𝐼| = 1 + | ®𝐽|, we may resort to the i.h., obtaining that there

Lemma B.32 (Reconstruction of typing derivation). Let ®𝐼=
(𝐼1, . . . , 𝐼𝑛) be a sequence of inputs such that all metaterms appearing
in the sequence are pure, and let ®𝐴= (𝐴1, . . . ,𝐴𝑛) be a sequence
of types. Suppose that Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴and that 𝐴/ ®𝐼: ®𝐴⇛𝐵. Suppose
moreover that Γ ⊢𝐼𝑖: 𝐴𝑖holds whenever 1 ≤𝑖≤𝑛and 𝐼𝑖is a
metaterm. Then Γ ⊢𝑡®𝐼: 𝐵.
Proof. By induction on the derivation of the judgment 𝐴/ ®𝐼:
®𝐴⇛𝐵:
1. Suppose that the judgment is of the form 𝐴/ ∅: ∅⇛𝐴,
where ®𝐼= ∅and ®𝐴= ∅and 𝐵= 𝐴. Then immediately we
have that Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴holds by hypothesis.
2. Suppose that the judgment (𝐶⇒𝐷) / (𝑠, ®𝐽) : (𝐶, ®𝐷) ⇛𝐵
is derived from 𝐷/ ®𝐽: ®𝐷⇛𝐵, where 𝐴= (𝐶⇒𝐷) and
®𝐼= (𝑠, ®𝐽) and ®𝐴= (𝐶, ®𝐷). Then we know by hypothesis that
Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐶⇒𝐷and also that Γ ⊢𝑠: 𝐶, so by the elimination
of the implication we have Γ ⊢𝑡𝑠: 𝐷. Applying the i.h. on
the judgments Γ ⊢𝑡𝑠: 𝐷and 𝐷/ ®𝐽: ®𝐷⇛𝐵, we obtain that
Γ ⊢𝑡𝑠®𝐽: 𝐵, that is, Γ ⊢𝑡®𝐼: 𝐵.
3. Suppose that the judgment (∀𝑎.𝐶) / (𝐷, ®𝐽) : (𝐷, ®𝐸) ⇛𝐵is
derived from 𝐶{𝑎:= 𝐷} / ®𝐽: ®𝐸⇛𝐵, where 𝐴= (∀𝑎.𝐶)
and ®𝐼= (𝐷, ®𝐽) and ®𝐴= (𝐷, ®𝐸). Then we know by hypothesis
that Γ ⊢𝑡: ∀𝑎.𝐶so by the elimination of the second-order
universal quantifier we have Γ ⊢𝑡𝐷: 𝐶{𝑎:= 𝐷}. Applying
the i.h. on the judgments Γ ⊢𝑡𝐷: 𝐶{𝑎:= 𝐷} and 𝐶{𝑎:=
𝐷} / ®𝐽: ®𝐸⇛𝐵, we obtain that Γ ⊢𝑡𝐷®𝐽: 𝐵, that is, Γ ⊢𝑡®𝐼:
𝐵.
□
Theorem B.33 (Completeness). If ⟨𝐴⟩𝑀Γ ↠★and 𝑀is good,

form such that 𝑀𝑥↠𝑀↓𝑥↠𝑁↓, and |𝑀↓|Γ⊢𝐴= |𝑀↓|Γ⊢𝐵⇒𝐶≥
|𝑁↓|Γ,𝑥:𝐵⊢𝐶= |𝑁↓|Δ⊢𝐶. We claim that we are now in a position to
apply the i.h. on ⟨𝐶⟩((𝑁↓)Δ):
• First, from the fact that 𝑀𝑥↠𝑁↓, we obtain that (𝑀𝑥)Δ ↠
(𝑁↓)Δ, and since we know that ⟨𝐶⟩((𝑀𝑥)Δ) = ⟨𝐶⟩((𝑀𝑥)Γ,𝑥:𝐵) =
⟨𝐶⟩(𝑀Γ ✠𝐵) ↠★, by confluence (Thm. 4.3) we obtain that
⟨𝐶⟩((𝑁↓)Δ) = ⟨𝐶⟩((𝑁↓)Γ,𝑥:𝐶) ↠★.
• Second, note that the pair (|𝑀↓|Γ⊢𝐴, |𝐴|) = (|𝑀↓|Γ⊢𝐴, |𝐵⇒
𝐶|) is strictly greater than (|𝑁↓|Δ⊢𝐶, |𝐶|) according to the
lexicographic order, because we have already shown that
|𝑀↓|Γ⊢𝐴≥|𝑁↓|Δ⊢𝐶and moreover |𝐵⇒𝐶| > |𝐶|.
Hence by i.h. we obtain that Δ ⊢𝑁↓: 𝐶. This means that Γ,𝑥:
𝐵⊢𝑁↓: 𝐶, so using the introduction rule for the implication we
have that Γ ⊢𝜆𝑥. 𝑁↓: 𝐵⇒𝐶. To conclude, note that 𝜆𝑥. 𝑁↓↞𝛽
𝜆𝑥. 𝑀↓𝑥→𝜂𝑀↓. Since →𝛽and →𝜂commute, and 𝑀↓is in 𝛽-
normal form, we have that 𝜆𝑥. 𝑁↓↠𝜂𝑀↓. Hence, by subject re-
duction Γ ⊢𝑀↓: 𝐵⇒𝐶.

C
Second-Order Logic — Properties of 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪
C.1
Higher-Order: Soundness (Thm. 5.2)
In this section, we show that 𝜆F𝜔is sound with respect to the
epistemic realizability semantics induced by 𝜆F𝜔
𝔪.
A straightforward observation is that types have unique kinds
under the global kind-assignment, i.e. if Ξ ⊢𝐴: 𝐾1 and Ξ ⊢𝐴: 𝐾2
then 𝐾1 = 𝐾2.
Lemma C.1 (Reduction preserves well-kindedness). If wk(𝑀)
and 𝑀→𝑀′ then wk(𝑀′).
Definition C.2 (Sets of valid expressions). We write K for the
set of all kinds. A type is ground if it has no free type-variables
(but note that it may have free eigenvariables). We write T for
the set of valid type/kind pairs, defined as T := {(𝐴, 𝐾) | Ξ ⊢
𝐴: 𝐾}. The subset of valid ground type/kind pairs is defined by
T0 := {(𝐴, 𝐾) | 𝐴is ground ∧(𝐴, 𝐾) ∈T}. We write T𝐾for the
set of valid types of kind 𝐾, defined as T𝐾:= {𝐴| Ξ ⊢𝐴: 𝐾}.
The subset of valid ground types of kind 𝐾is defined by T0
𝐾:=
{𝐴| 𝐴is ground ∧Ξ ⊢𝐴: 𝐾}. Sometimes we write “𝐴: 𝐾” instead
of “(𝐴, 𝐾)” for elements of T and T0. We write M for the set of all
well-kinded metaterms, i.e. {𝑀| wk(𝑀)}.
Definition C.3 (Dependent function space). If 𝐼is a set of indices
and (𝐽𝑖)𝑖∈𝐼is an indexed family of sets, we write Î
𝑖∈𝐼𝐽𝑖for the set
of all functions 𝑓: 𝐼→∪𝑖∈𝐼𝐽𝑖such that 𝑓(𝑖) ∈𝐽𝑖.

Lemma C.9 (Irrelevance). Let (VC𝑖)𝑖∈T0 be a candidate-family.

Definition C.12 (Substitutions and compatibility). A substitution
is a function 𝛾mapping each variable 𝑥to a well-kinded metaterm
𝛾(𝑥) ∈M. If 𝑀∈M is a well-kinded metaterm, we write 𝑀𝛾for the
well-kinded metaterm that results from replacing each occurrence
of each free variable 𝑥in 𝑀by 𝛾(𝑥), avoiding capture.
If 𝜂is a candidate-assignment, Γ is a environment, and 𝛾is a
substitution, we say that 𝛾is compatible with Γ under 𝜂, written
Γ ⊨𝜂𝛾, if for every 𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ such that 𝐴∈T𝐾we have that
𝑥𝛾∈J𝐴: 𝐾K𝜂.
Lemma C.13 (Adeqacy for terms). Let (VC𝑖)𝑖∈T0 be a candidate-
family, and let 𝜂be a candidate-assignment. Let 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴where
𝐴∈T𝐾and Γ ⊨𝜂𝛾. Assume moreover that 𝜉is compatible with the
global kind-assignment, i.e. 𝜉⊨Ξ (which can always be fulfilled by
appropriately renaming variables). Then 𝑡𝜂𝛾∈J𝐴: 𝐾K𝜂.
Proof. By induction on the derivation of the judgment 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡:
𝐴. The proof is exactly analogous to the proof in the second-order
case, but now using the higher-order versions of the irrelevance
lemma (Thm. C.9), the substitution lemma (Thm. C.10), and the
adequacy lemma for types (Thm. C.8). All rules in the higher-order
case follow the same structure reasoning than in the second order
case, except for the conversion rule, which is new. In the case of
the conversion rule, let 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐵be derived from 𝜉; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴and
𝐴=𝛽𝐵. By i.h., 𝑡𝜂𝛾∈J𝐴: PropK𝜂so by Thm. C.11 we conclude
that 𝑡𝜂𝛾∈J𝐵: PropK𝜂.
□
The following result constructs one specific candidate-family:
Proposition C.14. The following indexed family (Verif𝑖)𝑖∈T0,
defined by induction on the kind, is a candidate-family:

definition of candidate-families (Thm. C.4, item 5).

set of all functions

Theorem C.15 (Soundness). Let Γ,𝑡,𝐴be such that they have
no free type-variables. If Ξ; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴then ⟨𝐴⟩𝑡Γ ↠★.
Proof. For each kind 𝐾suppose that a𝐾is a eigenvariable such
that a𝐾∈T0
𝐾. Let F = (Verif𝑖)𝑖∈T0 be the candidate-family of
Thm. C.14. Define the following mapping:
𝜂(𝑎) := (a𝐾, La𝐾M)
whenever Ξ(𝑎) = 𝐾
where La𝐾M ∈Verifa𝐾:𝐾is given by the construction in Thm. C.14.
Observe that 𝜂is a F -candidate-assignment.
Note that Γ ⊨Γ, where Γ is the generative substitution for Γ,
because if 𝑥: 𝐴∈Γ then ⟨𝐴⟩✠𝐴↠𝑤★by correctness (Thm. C.5).
Note that the fact that the judgment Ξ; Γ ⊢𝑡: 𝐴holds implies
that Ξ ⊢𝐴: Prop, and moreover 𝐴is ground by hypothesis, so 𝐴∈
T0
Prop. Then by Thm. C.13 we have that 𝑡𝜂Γ ∈J𝐴: PropK𝜂, where
moreover J𝐴: PropK𝜂∈Verif𝐴𝜂:Prop by Thm. C.8. By the definition
of the family F in Thm. C.14, this means that ⟨𝐴𝜂⟩𝑡𝜂Γ ↠𝑤★. Since
𝐴and 𝑡have no free type-variables, 𝐴𝜂= 𝐴and 𝑡𝜂= 𝑡. Hence
⟨𝐴⟩𝑡↠𝑤★, as required.
□
